========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Doctor, I suppose I'm here because things have gotten out of hand. What scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just keep reaching for more... even while I’m telling myself, *Tom, stop this right now.* It’s like I have no control at all. 

You know, it reminds me of my old Ford pickup I had back in '75. Beautiful truck, cherry red. But the brakes on that thing were shot. I remember driving down Miller's Hill—steepest road in town, they don't make roads like that anymore—and I was stomping on the pedal, but the truck just kept rolling. Terrifying feeling. My neighbor, Frank, he told me I should've replaced the pads sooner, but Frank always thought he knew everything. He had this little poodle that used to bark at the mailman... but anyway, yeah, it's that same feeling. Just rolling down the hill and I can't stop my hands from grabbing more food, no matter how hard I tell myself to quit.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose I do, yeah. It's like... it's like I'm shoveling it in before I even realize what I'm doing. Fast, just really fast. My wife, Mary—God rest her soul—she used to say I ate like a man who hadn't seen a hot meal in a week. She was a slow eater, Mary was. Took her time with every bite. We'd go to this little diner on Main Street, used to be called Sal's, I think it's a hardware store now. Sal made the best meatloaf, and Mary would just sit there, cutting it into these tiny little pieces. I'd be done with my whole plate and half a slice of cherry pie before she even finished her vegetables. She always said, "Tom, the food ain't running away." But during these... these times now, it's not like that. It's not just eating fast because I'm hungry. It's a different kind of fast. But Mary, she always knew how to make a meal last an hour...

Well, yes, they do. I've been keeping track, you see, writing it all down in this little blue notebook my daughter got me. She bought it at that stationery shop downtown, the one next to the old movie theater. I remember when they used to show double features there for a quarter. Saw 'The Bridge on the River Kwai' there... magnificent picture. But anyway, my logs in that notebook, they show that for months now, almost every single week, there's at least one afternoon where I just lose it. Eat everything in sight. And then... well, then I panic. I try to fix it. I make myself sick, or take laxatives, or I just don't eat anything at all the next day. Sometimes I just push myself to the bone, walking miles around the neighborhood or doing heavy yard work until my back aches. Which reminds me, the gutters on my house are just full of leaves again. That oak tree in the front yard drops them everywhere. I told the kid down the street I'd pay him ten bucks to clear them out, but kids these days, they don't want to work. Back when I was his age, I'd have jumped at ten bucks. I used to deliver papers, rain or shine... but yes, the eating and then trying to undo it, it's been happening pretty much every week for a long while now.

We'd go to this little diner on Main Street, used to be called Sal's, I think it's a hardware store now. Sal made the best meatloaf, and Mary would just sit there, cutting it into these tiny little pieces. I'd be done with my whole plate and half a slice of cherry pie before she even finished her vegetables. She always said, \"Tom, the food ain't running away.\" But during these... these times now, it's not like that. It's not just eating fast because I'm hungry. It's a different kind of fast. But Mary, she always knew how to make a meal last an hour...\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating without control happen to you regularly?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do. I've been keeping track, you see, writing it all down in this little blue notebook my daughter got me. She bought it at that stationery shop downtown, the one next to the old movie theater. I remember when they used to show double features there for a quarter. Saw 'The Bridge on the River Kwai' there... magnificent picture. But anyway, my logs in that notebook, they show that for months now, almost every single week, there's at least one afternoon where I just lose it. Eat everything in sight. And then... well, then I panic. I try to fix it. I make myself sick, or take laxatives, or I just don't eat anything at all the next day. Sometimes I just push myself to the bone, walking miles around the neighborhood or doing heavy yard work until my back aches. Which reminds me, the gutters on my house are just full of leaves again. That oak tree in the front yard drops them everywhere. I told the kid down the street I'd pay him ten bucks to clear them out, but kids these days, they don't want to work. Back when I was his age, I'd have jumped at ten bucks. I used to deliver papers, rain or shine... but yes, the eating and then trying to undo it, it's been happening pretty much every week for a long while now.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you worry about gaining weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, I suppose I do worry about it, yes. Weight's a funny thing, isn't it? You know, back when I was working at the plant—forty years I put in there, stamping metal parts—I never had to worry about my weight. I was on my feet all day, sweating in the summer... the ventilation in that place was terrible. My buddy Charlie used to bring in this big industrial fan, but it just blew the hot air around. Charlie was a big guy, though. He always had a gut on him, even when we were young. He used to eat these massive hero sandwiches for lunch, wrapped in that thick white paper. I wonder whatever happened to Charlie... he moved out to Arizona, I think. Said the dry heat was better for his joints. My knees have been giving me some trouble lately, actually. The doctor gave me some cream for it, but it smells like wintergreen. Reminds me of those little pink candies my grandmother used to keep in a glass dish on her coffee table. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. I guess I just try not to think about it too much, but it's hard not to when you feel so full.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, guilt... I don't know if guilt is exactly the word for it. It's more just this awful, heavy feeling, you know? Like I've done something I shouldn't have. Speaking of things I shouldn't have done, I bought this new lawnmower last week. One of those fancy self-propelled ones. The salesman at Sears—well, it wasn't Sears, Sears closed down years ago. It was that big box store out on Highway 9. Anyway, the young fella there talked me into it. Said it would be easier on my back. But the darn thing is so complicated to start! You have to prime it three times, hold down this safety bar, and pull the cord all at once. My old push mower, you just gave it a yank and it roared to life. Had that old Briggs & Stratton engine. Built like a tank, that thing was. I used to mow the whole neighborhood's lawns when I was a teenager to save up for my first car. A '57 Chevy Bel Air. Two-tone blue and white. Beautiful machine. Wish I still had it, they go for a fortune at those classic car auctions now. I watch them on the television sometimes on Sunday afternoons when there's no baseball on. But... yeah, I'm not really sure if I'd call it guilt after the eating. Just... bad. I feel bad.

Unable to stop... yes, that's exactly it, and that's the worst part of the whole thing. What scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just have a mind of their own. The doctor gave me some cream for it, but it smells like wintergreen. Reminds me of those little pink candies my grandmother used to keep in a glass dish on her coffee table. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. I guess I just try not to think about it too much, but it's hard not to when you feel so full.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, guilt... I don't know if guilt is exactly the word for it. It's more just this awful, heavy feeling, you know? Like I've done something I shouldn't have. Speaking of things I shouldn't have done, I bought this new lawnmower last week. One of those fancy self-propelled ones. The salesman at Sears—well, it wasn't Sears, Sears closed down years ago. It was that big box store out on Highway 9. Anyway, the young fella there talked me into it. Said it would be easier on my back. But the darn thing is so complicated to start! You have to prime it three times, hold down this safety bar, and pull the cord all at once. My old push mower, you just gave it a yank and it roared to life. Had that old Briggs & Stratton engine. Built like a tank, that thing was. I used to mow the whole neighborhood's lawns when I was a teenager to save up for my first car. A '57 Chevy Bel Air. Two-tone blue and white. Beautiful machine. Wish I still had it, they go for a fortune at those classic car auctions now. I watch them on the television sometimes on Sunday afternoons when there's no baseball on. But... yeah, I'm not really sure if I'd call it guilt after the eating. Just... bad. I feel bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Unable to stop... yes, that's exactly it, and that's the worst part of the whole thing. What scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just have a mind of their own. They just keep reaching for more, even while I’m sitting there telling myself, Tom, stop this right now. But they just keep moving. \n\nMy hands, I mean. They aren't as quick as they used to be, though. The arthritis really gets into the knuckles these days, especially when it rains. And we've had a lot of rain this spring. More than usual, the weatherman on Channel 4 was saying just the other night. I like him, that young fellow with the bright ties. He always wears a different tie. My wife, God rest her soul, she used to buy me a new tie every Father's Day. I had quite the collection going by the end. Silk ones, wool ones, even one with little mallard ducks on it. I actually wore that duck tie to my retirement dinner. The boys at the plant threw it for me at the Italian place down on Main Street, the one with the red checkered tablecloths. Best garlic bread I ever had, though I can't eat garlic like I used to. Gives me terrible heartburn now. I have to sleep propped up on three pillows if I even look at a garlic clove... but anyway, what was the question again?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your fear of gaining weight is what triggers these episodes of eating without control?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose... I mean, yes, the weight is a big part of it. It's funny you mention the fear, because my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he's a nice enough fellow, a bit young, maybe just out of medical school—he keeps telling me I'm too thin. He looks at my chart and shakes his head. But he doesn't understand. If I step on that scale in the bathroom and it's up even half a pound, I just... I feel this panic in my chest. I feel so ashamed, like I've completely let myself go and I have to fix it right that second. 

Speaking of fixing things, the scale itself is getting a bit wonky. It's one of those old analog ones, you know, with the little dial that spins around. I bought it at a hardware store back in... oh, it must have been 1982. The same year I put the new roof on the house. We used asphalt shingles, the heavy-duty ones. The roofer said they'd last thirty years, and he wasn't lying, though the wind took a few off during that big storm we had last October. The one that knocked the power out for three days. I had to use a flashlight to get around the house, and the batteries kept dying. I always buy the cheap batteries at the dollar store, but my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she swears by the name-brand ones. She brought over a casserole when the power was out. Tuna noodle, I think it was. It had those little crushed potato chips on top. I used to love those when I was a kid... but, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the scale. Yes, if it goes up even a little, I just panic.

What scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just keep reaching for more... even while I’m telling myself, *Tom, stop this right now.* It’s like I have no control at all. \n\nYou know, it reminds me of my old Ford pickup I had back in '75. Beautiful truck, cherry red. But the brakes on that thing were shot. I remember driving down Miller's Hill—steepest road in town, they don't make roads like that anymore—and I was stomping on the pedal, but the truck just kept rolling. Terrifying feeling. My neighbor, Frank, he told me I should've replaced the pads sooner, but Frank always thought he knew everything. He had this little poodle that used to bark at the mailman... but anyway, yeah, it's that same feeling. Just rolling down the hill and I can't stop my hands from grabbing more food, no matter how hard I tell myself to quit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yeah. It's like... it's like I'm shoveling it in before I even realize what I'm doing. Fast, just really fast. My wife, Mary—God rest her soul—she used to say I ate like a man who hadn't seen a hot meal in a week. She was a slow eater, Mary was. Took her time with every bite. We'd go to this little diner on Main Street, used to be called Sal's, I think it's a hardware store now. Sal made the best meatloaf, and Mary would just sit there, cutting it into these tiny little pieces. I'd be done with my whole plate and half a slice of cherry pie before she even finished her vegetables. She always said, \"Tom, the food ain't running away.\" But during these... these times now, it's not like that. It's not just eating fast because I'm hungry. It's a different kind of fast. But Mary, she always knew how to make a meal last an hour...\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating without control happen to you regularly?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do. I've been keeping track, you see, writing it all down in this little blue notebook my daughter got me. She bought it at that stationery shop downtown, the one next to the old movie theater. I remember when they used to show double features there for a quarter. Saw 'The Bridge on the River Kwai' there... magnificent picture. But anyway, my logs in that notebook, they show that for months now, almost every single week, there's at least one afternoon where I just lose it. Eat everything in sight. And then... well, then I panic. I try to fix it. I make myself sick, or take laxatives, or I just don't eat anything at all the next day. Sometimes I just push myself to the bone, walking miles around the neighborhood or doing heavy yard work until my back aches. Which reminds me, the gutters on my house are just full of leaves again. That oak tree in the front yard drops them everywhere. I told the kid down the street I'd pay him ten bucks to clear them out, but kids these days, they don't want to work. Back when I was his age, I'd have jumped at ten bucks. I used to deliver papers, rain or shine... but yes, the eating and then trying to undo it, it's been happening pretty much every week for a long while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do worry about it, yes. Wish I still had it, they go for a fortune at those classic car auctions now. I watch them on the television sometimes on Sunday afternoons when there's no baseball on. But... yeah, I'm not really sure if I'd call it guilt after the eating. Just... bad. Well, yes, I... I do. After I eat like that, I'll go into the bathroom and, you know, make myself bring it all back up. Sometimes I'll even take laxatives, too, because I just get so terrified that the food is going to stay inside me and turn into fat. 

Speaking of the bathroom, I had to call the plumber out just last Tuesday. The sink was draining so slow, you wouldn't believe it. Took ten minutes just for the water to go down after I shaved. I use one of those old safety razors, you know, the ones where you have to put the double-edged blade in yourself. My father taught me how to shave with one of those when I was fifteen. He always used Williams Mug shaving soap and a badger hair brush. Smelled like... well, it smelled like my dad. He worked at the lumber yard down by the train tracks for forty years. Hard worker, he was. Never took a sick day. The trains used to rattle the windows of our house when they went by at night. I found it soothing, actually, like a lullaby. The freight trains were the longest. You'd count the cars—fifty, sixty, sometimes seventy cars long, carrying coal and lumber and who knows what else. They don't run as many trains on that line anymore. It's mostly trucks now. Big eighteen-wheelers tearing up the highway. The noise they make on the interstate is just awful, especially when they use those engine brakes. Jake brakes, I think my nephew called them. He drives a truck. Hauls produce from California all the way to New York. Brings me a crate of fresh oranges sometimes when he passes through. Best oranges you'll ever taste, so sweet and juicy... but, um, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, I do make myself throw up, or take the laxatives. I just can't stand the thought of it staying in me.

The sink was draining so slow, you wouldn't believe it. Took ten minutes just for the water to go down after I shaved. I use one of those old safety razors, you know, the ones where you have to put the double-edged blade in yourself. My father taught me how to shave with one of those when I was fifteen. He always used Williams Mug shaving soap and a badger hair brush. Smelled like... well, it smelled like my dad. He worked at the lumber yard down by the train tracks for forty years. Hard worker, he was. Never took a sick day. The trains used to rattle the windows of our house when they went by at night. I found it soothing, actually, like a lullaby. The freight trains were the longest. You'd count the cars—fifty, sixty, sometimes seventy cars long, carrying coal and lumber and who knows what else. They don't run as many trains on that line anymore. It's mostly trucks now. Big eighteen-wheelers tearing up the highway. The noise they make on the interstate is just awful, especially when they use those engine brakes. Jake brakes, I think my nephew called them. He drives a truck. Hauls produce from California all the way to New York. Brings me a crate of fresh oranges sometimes when he passes through. Best oranges you'll ever taste, so sweet and juicy... but, um, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, I do make myself throw up, or take the laxatives. I just can't stand the thought of it staying in me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sometimes I just push myself to the bone, walking miles around the neighborhood or doing heavy yard work until my back aches. Which reminds me, the gutters on my house are just full of leaves again. That oak tree in the front yard drops them everywhere. I told the kid down the street I'd pay him ten bucks to clear them out, but kids these days, they don't want to work. Back when I was his age, I'd have jumped at ten bucks. I used to deliver papers, rain or shine... but yes, the eating and then trying to undo it, it's been happening pretty much every week for a long while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do worry about it, yes. Weight's a funny thing, isn't it? You know, back when I was working at the plant—forty years I put in there, stamping metal parts—I never had to worry about my weight. I was on my feet all day, sweating in the summer... the ventilation in that place was terrible. My buddy Charlie used to bring in this big industrial fan, but it just blew the hot air around. Charlie was a big guy, though. He always had a gut on him, even when we were young. He used to eat these massive hero sandwiches for lunch, wrapped in that thick white paper. I wonder whatever happened to Charlie... he moved out to Arizona, I think. Said the dry heat was better for his joints. My knees have been giving me some trouble lately, actually. The doctor gave me some cream for it, but it smells like wintergreen. Reminds me of those little pink candies my grandmother used to keep in a glass dish on her coffee table. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. I guess I just try not to think about it too much, but it's hard not to when you feel so full.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, guilt... It was that big box store out on Highway 9. Anyway, the young fella there talked me into it. Said it would be easier on my back. But the darn thing is so complicated to start! You have to prime it three times, hold down this safety bar, and pull the cord all at once. My old push mower, you just gave it a yank and it roared to life. Had that old Briggs & Stratton engine. Built like a tank, that thing was. I used to mow the whole neighborhood's lawns when I was a teenager to save up for my first car. A '57 Chevy Bel Air. Two-tone blue and white. Beautiful machine. Wish I still had it, they go for a fortune at those classic car auctions now. I watch them on the television sometimes on Sunday afternoons when there's no baseball on. But... yeah, I'm not really sure if I'd call it guilt after the eating. Just... bad. My wife, God rest her soul, she used to buy me a new tie every Father's Day. I had quite the collection going by the end. Silk ones, wool ones, even one with little mallard ducks on it. I actually wore that duck tie to my retirement dinner. The boys at the plant threw it for me at the Italian place down on Main Street, the one with the red checkered tablecloths. Best garlic bread I ever had, though I can't eat garlic like I used to. Gives me terrible heartburn now. I have to sleep propped up on three pillows if I even look at a garlic clove... but anyway, what was the question again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what triggers these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, that's exactly why I do it. I mean, I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it all back up, or I take those laxatives, because I'm just so scared that if I don't, all that food will just stay right there in my stomach and turn into fat. I just can't have that. 

Though, you know, speaking of stomachs, mine has been a bit upset lately anyway. I think it might be that new brand of coffee I bought at the supermarket. I usually stick to Folgers, the classic roast in the red plastic tub. My wife always bought Folgers. She'd set the percolator up every night before bed, so all we had to do was plug it in when we woke up. The smell of that coffee brewing in the morning... it was heavenly. We had this little breakfast nook in our old house, with a bay window that looked out over the backyard. We had a bird feeder out there, a wooden one shaped like a little barn. The blue jays were always bullying the smaller birds away from the seeds. Nasty things, blue jays, though they are pretty to look at. My grandson, little Jimmy—well, he's not so little anymore, he's in college now, studying engineering of all things—he used to love watching those birds. He'd sit there with his nose pressed against the glass, pointing at the cardinals and the chickadees. He wants to build bridges, he says. Or maybe airplanes, I can't quite remember. He's a smart boy, gets it from his mother's side. My daughter was always top of her class in math... but, oh, what was the question? Right, the weight. Yes, I do it to stop the food from staying inside me, to stop the weight.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, I suppose I'm here because things have gotten out of hand. What scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just keep reaching for more... even while I’m telling myself, *Tom, stop this right now.* It’s like I have no control at all. \n\nYou know, it reminds me of my old Ford pickup I had back in '75. Beautiful truck, cherry red. But the brakes on that thing were shot. I remember driving down Miller's Hill—steepest road in town, they don't make roads like that anymore—and I was stomping on the pedal, but the truck just kept rolling. Terrifying feeling. My neighbor, Frank, he told me I should've replaced the pads sooner, but Frank always thought he knew everything. He had this little poodle that used to bark at the mailman... but anyway, yeah, it's that same feeling. Just rolling down the hill and I can't stop my hands from grabbing more food, no matter how hard I tell myself to quit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yeah. It's like... it's like I'm shoveling it in before I even realize what I'm doing. Fast, just really fast. My wife, Mary—God rest her soul—she used to say I ate like a man who hadn't seen a hot meal in a week. She was a slow eater, Mary was. Took her time with every bite. We'd go to this little diner on Main Street, used to be called Sal's, I think it's a hardware store now. Sal made the best meatloaf, and Mary would just sit there, cutting it into these tiny little pieces. I'd be done with my whole plate and half a slice of cherry pie before she even finished her vegetables. She always said, \"Tom, the food ain't running away.\" But during these... these times now, it's not like that. It's not just eating fast because I'm hungry. It's a different kind of fast. But Mary, she always knew how to make a meal last an hour...\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating without control happen to you regularly?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do. I've been keeping track, you see, writing it all down in this little blue notebook my daughter got me. She bought it at that stationery shop downtown, the one next to the old movie theater. I remember when they used to show double features there for a quarter. Saw 'The Bridge on the River Kwai' there... magnificent picture. But anyway, my logs in that notebook, they show that for months now, almost every single week, there's at least one afternoon where I just lose it. Eat everything in sight. And then... well, then I panic. I try to fix it. I make myself sick, or take laxatives, or I just don't eat anything at all the next day. Sometimes I just push myself to the bone, walking miles around the neighborhood or doing heavy yard work until my back aches. Which reminds me, the gutters on my house are just full of leaves again. That oak tree in the front yard drops them everywhere. I told the kid down the street I'd pay him ten bucks to clear them out, but kids these days, they don't want to work. Back when I was his age, I'd have jumped at ten bucks. I used to deliver papers, rain or shine... but yes, the eating and then trying to undo it, it's been happening pretty much every week for a long while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do worry about it, yes. Weight's a funny thing, isn't it? You know, back when I was working at the plant—forty years I put in there, stamping metal parts—I never had to worry about my weight. I was on my feet all day, sweating in the summer... the ventilation in that place was terrible. My buddy Charlie used to bring in this big industrial fan, but it just blew the hot air around. Charlie was a big guy, though. He always had a gut on him, even when we were young. He used to eat these massive hero sandwiches for lunch, wrapped in that thick white paper. I wonder whatever happened to Charlie... he moved out to Arizona, I think. Said the dry heat was better for his joints. My knees have been giving me some trouble lately, actually. The doctor gave me some cream for it, but it smells like wintergreen. Reminds me of those little pink candies my grandmother used to keep in a glass dish on her coffee table. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. I guess I just try not to think about it too much, but it's hard not to when you feel so full.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, guilt... I don't know if guilt is exactly the word for it. It's more just this awful, heavy feeling, you know? Like I've done something I shouldn't have. Speaking of things I shouldn't have done, I bought this new lawnmower last week. One of those fancy self-propelled ones. The salesman at Sears—well, it wasn't Sears, Sears closed down years ago. It was that big box store out on Highway 9. Anyway, the young fella there talked me into it. Said it would be easier on my back. But the darn thing is so complicated to start! You have to prime it three times, hold down this safety bar, and pull the cord all at once. My old push mower, you just gave it a yank and it roared to life. Had that old Briggs & Stratton engine. Built like a tank, that thing was. I used to mow the whole neighborhood's lawns when I was a teenager to save up for my first car. A '57 Chevy Bel Air. Two-tone blue and white. Beautiful machine. Wish I still had it, they go for a fortune at those classic car auctions now. I watch them on the television sometimes on Sunday afternoons when there's no baseball on. But... yeah, I'm not really sure if I'd call it guilt after the eating. Just... bad. I feel bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Unable to stop... yes, that's exactly it, and that's the worst part of the whole thing. What scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just have a mind of their own. They just keep reaching for more, even while I’m sitting there telling myself, Tom, stop this right now. But they just keep moving. \n\nMy hands, I mean. They aren't as quick as they used to be, though. The arthritis really gets into the knuckles these days, especially when it rains. And we've had a lot of rain this spring. More than usual, the weatherman on Channel 4 was saying just the other night. I like him, that young fellow with the bright ties. He always wears a different tie. My wife, God rest her soul, she used to buy me a new tie every Father's Day. I had quite the collection going by the end. Silk ones, wool ones, even one with little mallard ducks on it. I actually wore that duck tie to my retirement dinner. The boys at the plant threw it for me at the Italian place down on Main Street, the one with the red checkered tablecloths. Best garlic bread I ever had, though I can't eat garlic like I used to. Gives me terrible heartburn now. I have to sleep propped up on three pillows if I even look at a garlic clove... but anyway, what was the question again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what triggers these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, yes, the weight is a big part of it. It's funny you mention the fear, because my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he's a nice enough fellow, a bit young, maybe just out of medical school—he keeps telling me I'm too thin. He looks at my chart and shakes his head. But he doesn't understand. If I step on that scale in the bathroom and it's up even half a pound, I just... I feel this panic in my chest. I feel so ashamed, like I've completely let myself go and I have to fix it right that second. \n\nSpeaking of fixing things, the scale itself is getting a bit wonky. It's one of those old analog ones, you know, with the little dial that spins around. I bought it at a hardware store back in... oh, it must have been 1982. The same year I put the new roof on the house. We used asphalt shingles, the heavy-duty ones. The roofer said they'd last thirty years, and he wasn't lying, though the wind took a few off during that big storm we had last October. The one that knocked the power out for three days. I had to use a flashlight to get around the house, and the batteries kept dying. I always buy the cheap batteries at the dollar store, but my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she swears by the name-brand ones. She brought over a casserole when the power was out. Tuna noodle, I think it was. It had those little crushed potato chips on top. I used to love those when I was a kid... but, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the scale. Yes, if it goes up even a little, I just panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to make yourself vomit after these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I do. After I eat like that, I'll go into the bathroom and, you know, make myself bring it all back up. Sometimes I'll even take laxatives, too, because I just get so terrified that the food is going to stay inside me and turn into fat. \n\nSpeaking of the bathroom, I had to call the plumber out just last Tuesday. The sink was draining so slow, you wouldn't believe it. Took ten minutes just for the water to go down after I shaved. I use one of those old safety razors, you know, the ones where you have to put the double-edged blade in yourself. My father taught me how to shave with one of those when I was fifteen. He always used Williams Mug shaving soap and a badger hair brush. Smelled like... well, it smelled like my dad. He worked at the lumber yard down by the train tracks for forty years. Hard worker, he was. Never took a sick day. The trains used to rattle the windows of our house when they went by at night. I found it soothing, actually, like a lullaby. The freight trains were the longest. You'd count the cars—fifty, sixty, sometimes seventy cars long, carrying coal and lumber and who knows what else. They don't run as many trains on that line anymore. It's mostly trucks now. Big eighteen-wheelers tearing up the highway. The noise they make on the interstate is just awful, especially when they use those engine brakes. Jake brakes, I think my nephew called them. He drives a truck. Hauls produce from California all the way to New York. Brings me a crate of fresh oranges sometimes when he passes through. Best oranges you'll ever taste, so sweet and juicy... but, um, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, I do make myself throw up, or take the laxatives. I just can't stand the thought of it staying in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think making yourself vomit or using laxatives after eating helps prevent you from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that's exactly why I do it. I mean, I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it all back up, or I take those laxatives, because I'm just so scared that if I don't, all that food will just stay right there in my stomach and turn into fat. I just can't have that. \n\nThough, you know, speaking of stomachs, mine has been a bit upset lately anyway. I think it might be that new brand of coffee I bought at the supermarket. I usually stick to Folgers, the classic roast in the red plastic tub. My wife always bought Folgers. She'd set the percolator up every night before bed, so all we had to do was plug it in when we woke up. The smell of that coffee brewing in the morning... it was heavenly. We had this little breakfast nook in our old house, with a bay window that looked out over the backyard. We had a bird feeder out there, a wooden one shaped like a little barn. The blue jays were always bullying the smaller birds away from the seeds. Nasty things, blue jays, though they are pretty to look at. My grandson, little Jimmy—well, he's not so little anymore, he's in college now, studying engineering of all things—he used to love watching those birds. He'd sit there with his nose pressed against the glass, pointing at the cardinals and the chickadees. He wants to build bridges, he says. Or maybe airplanes, I can't quite remember. He's a smart boy, gets it from his mother's side. My daughter was always top of her class in math... but, oh, what was the question? Right, the weight. Yes, I do it to stop the food from staying inside me, to stop the weight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not just those things. I mean, after a really bad eating spell, I'll... I'll make myself skip breakfast and lunch the next day, just drink black coffee. And then I'll go out and keep walking laps around the neighborhood, or I'll do yard chores, just raking and weeding long after my knees and my back are screaming at me to stop. I just have to burn it off, you see. 

Speaking of yard chores, the dandelions this year are just out of control. I don't know what it is about the soil in my front yard, but they just spring up overnight like little yellow buttons. I tried that spray stuff, the one in the green bottle you hook up to the garden hose? Bought it at the hardware store downtown. The fellow who runs the place, Mr. Henderson, he's been there since I was a boy. He's got to be pushing ninety now, still working the cash register every day. He wears these thick glasses, the kind that make his eyes look huge. He told me the spray would work, but it just made the grass turn brown in patches. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins—the one who brought the tuna casserole—she told me I should just dig them out by the roots with a special tool. She ordered one from a catalog, one of those thick paper ones that comes in the mail. I used to love looking through the Sears Roebuck catalog when I was little. The toy section in the back was the best part. I wanted a shiny red wagon so badly one year, and my father actually got it for me for Christmas. We didn't have much money, so I know he must have saved up for months. I pulled that wagon everywhere. I used to give my little sister rides down the sidewalk... but, um, what were we talking about? Oh, right. The walking and the yard work. Yes, I do that to make up for the eating, too. Just push myself until I can't anymore.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, I suppose I'm here because things have gotten out of hand. What scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just keep reaching for more... even while I’m telling myself, *Tom, stop this right now.* It’s like I have no control at all. \n\nYou know, it reminds me of my old Ford pickup I had back in '75. Beautiful truck, cherry red. But the brakes on that thing were shot. I remember driving down Miller's Hill—steepest road in town, they don't make roads like that anymore—and I was stomping on the pedal, but the truck just kept rolling. Terrifying feeling. My neighbor, Frank, he told me I should've replaced the pads sooner, but Frank always thought he knew everything. He had this little poodle that used to bark at the mailman... but anyway, yeah, it's that same feeling. Just rolling down the hill and I can't stop my hands from grabbing more food, no matter how hard I tell myself to quit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yeah. It's like... it's like I'm shoveling it in before I even realize what I'm doing. Fast, just really fast. My wife, Mary—God rest her soul—she used to say I ate like a man who hadn't seen a hot meal in a week. She was a slow eater, Mary was. Took her time with every bite. We'd go to this little diner on Main Street, used to be called Sal's, I think it's a hardware store now. Sal made the best meatloaf, and Mary would just sit there, cutting it into these tiny little pieces. I'd be done with my whole plate and half a slice of cherry pie before she even finished her vegetables. She always said, \"Tom, the food ain't running away.\" But during these... these times now, it's not like that. It's not just eating fast because I'm hungry. It's a different kind of fast. But Mary, she always knew how to make a meal last an hour...\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating without control happen to you regularly?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do. I've been keeping track, you see, writing it all down in this little blue notebook my daughter got me. She bought it at that stationery shop downtown, the one next to the old movie theater. I remember when they used to show double features there for a quarter. Saw 'The Bridge on the River Kwai' there... magnificent picture. But anyway, my logs in that notebook, they show that for months now, almost every single week, there's at least one afternoon where I just lose it. Eat everything in sight. And then... well, then I panic. I try to fix it. I make myself sick, or take laxatives, or I just don't eat anything at all the next day. Sometimes I just push myself to the bone, walking miles around the neighborhood or doing heavy yard work until my back aches. Which reminds me, the gutters on my house are just full of leaves again. That oak tree in the front yard drops them everywhere. I told the kid down the street I'd pay him ten bucks to clear them out, but kids these days, they don't want to work. Back when I was his age, I'd have jumped at ten bucks. I used to deliver papers, rain or shine... but yes, the eating and then trying to undo it, it's been happening pretty much every week for a long while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do worry about it, yes. Weight's a funny thing, isn't it? You know, back when I was working at the plant—forty years I put in there, stamping metal parts—I never had to worry about my weight. I was on my feet all day, sweating in the summer... the ventilation in that place was terrible. My buddy Charlie used to bring in this big industrial fan, but it just blew the hot air around. Charlie was a big guy, though. He always had a gut on him, even when we were young. He used to eat these massive hero sandwiches for lunch, wrapped in that thick white paper. I wonder whatever happened to Charlie... he moved out to Arizona, I think. Said the dry heat was better for his joints. My knees have been giving me some trouble lately, actually. The doctor gave me some cream for it, but it smells like wintergreen. Reminds me of those little pink candies my grandmother used to keep in a glass dish on her coffee table. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. I guess I just try not to think about it too much, but it's hard not to when you feel so full.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, guilt... I don't know if guilt is exactly the word for it. It's more just this awful, heavy feeling, you know? Like I've done something I shouldn't have. Speaking of things I shouldn't have done, I bought this new lawnmower last week. One of those fancy self-propelled ones. The salesman at Sears—well, it wasn't Sears, Sears closed down years ago. It was that big box store out on Highway 9. Anyway, the young fella there talked me into it. Said it would be easier on my back. But the darn thing is so complicated to start! You have to prime it three times, hold down this safety bar, and pull the cord all at once. My old push mower, you just gave it a yank and it roared to life. Had that old Briggs & Stratton engine. Built like a tank, that thing was. I used to mow the whole neighborhood's lawns when I was a teenager to save up for my first car. A '57 Chevy Bel Air. Two-tone blue and white. Beautiful machine. Wish I still had it, they go for a fortune at those classic car auctions now. I watch them on the television sometimes on Sunday afternoons when there's no baseball on. But... yeah, I'm not really sure if I'd call it guilt after the eating. Just... bad. I feel bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Unable to stop... yes, that's exactly it, and that's the worst part of the whole thing. What scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just have a mind of their own. They just keep reaching for more, even while I’m sitting there telling myself, Tom, stop this right now. But they just keep moving. \n\nMy hands, I mean. They aren't as quick as they used to be, though. The arthritis really gets into the knuckles these days, especially when it rains. And we've had a lot of rain this spring. More than usual, the weatherman on Channel 4 was saying just the other night. I like him, that young fellow with the bright ties. He always wears a different tie. My wife, God rest her soul, she used to buy me a new tie every Father's Day. I had quite the collection going by the end. Silk ones, wool ones, even one with little mallard ducks on it. I actually wore that duck tie to my retirement dinner. The boys at the plant threw it for me at the Italian place down on Main Street, the one with the red checkered tablecloths. Best garlic bread I ever had, though I can't eat garlic like I used to. Gives me terrible heartburn now. I have to sleep propped up on three pillows if I even look at a garlic clove... but anyway, what was the question again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what triggers these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... After I eat like that, I'll go into the bathroom and, you know, make myself bring it all back up. Sometimes I'll even take laxatives, too, because I just get so terrified that the food is going to stay inside me and turn into fat. \n\nSpeaking of the bathroom, I had to call the plumber out just last Tuesday. The sink was draining so slow, you wouldn't believe it. Took ten minutes just for the water to go down after I shaved. I use one of those old safety razors, you know, the ones where you have to put the double-edged blade in yourself. My father taught me how to shave with one of those when I was fifteen. He always used Williams Mug shaving soap and a badger hair brush. Smelled like... well, it smelled like my dad. He worked at the lumber yard down by the train tracks for forty years. Hard worker, he was. Never took a sick day. The trains used to rattle the windows of our house when they went by at night. I found it soothing, actually, like a lullaby. The freight trains were the longest. You'd count the cars—fifty, sixty, sometimes seventy cars long, carrying coal and lumber and who knows what else. They don't run as many trains on that line anymore. It's mostly trucks now. Big eighteen-wheelers tearing up the highway. The noise they make on the interstate is just awful, especially when they use those engine brakes. Jake brakes, I think my nephew called them. He drives a truck. Hauls produce from California all the way to New York. Brings me a crate of fresh oranges sometimes when he passes through. Best oranges you'll ever taste, so sweet and juicy... but, um, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, I do make myself throw up, or take the laxatives. I just can't stand the thought of it staying in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think making yourself vomit or using laxatives after eating helps prevent you from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that's exactly why I do it. I mean, I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it all back up, or I take those laxatives, because I'm just so scared that if I don't, all that food will just stay right there in my stomach and turn into fat. I just can't have that. \n\nThough, you know, speaking of stomachs, mine has been a bit upset lately anyway. I think it might be that new brand of coffee I bought at the supermarket. I usually stick to Folgers, the classic roast in the red plastic tub. My wife always bought Folgers. She'd set the percolator up every night before bed, so all we had to do was plug it in when we woke up. The smell of that coffee brewing in the morning... it was heavenly. We had this little breakfast nook in our old house, with a bay window that looked out over the backyard. We had a bird feeder out there, a wooden one shaped like a little barn. The blue jays were always bullying the smaller birds away from the seeds. Nasty things, blue jays, though they are pretty to look at. My grandson, little Jimmy—well, he's not so little anymore, he's in college now, studying engineering of all things—he used to love watching those birds. He'd sit there with his nose pressed against the glass, pointing at the cardinals and the chickadees. He wants to build bridges, he says. Or maybe airplanes, I can't quite remember. He's a smart boy, gets it from his mother's side. My daughter was always top of her class in math... but, oh, what was the question? Right, the weight. Yes, I do it to stop the food from staying inside me, to stop the weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your attempts to vomit or use laxatives after eating are the only things that keep you from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not just those things. I mean, after a really bad eating spell, I'll... I'll make myself skip breakfast and lunch the next day, just drink black coffee. And then I'll go out and keep walking laps around the neighborhood, or I'll do yard chores, just raking and weeding long after my knees and my back are screaming at me to stop. I just have to burn it off, you see. \n\nSpeaking of yard chores, the dandelions this year are just out of control. I don't know what it is about the soil in my front yard, but they just spring up overnight like little yellow buttons. I tried that spray stuff, the one in the green bottle you hook up to the garden hose? Bought it at the hardware store downtown. The fellow who runs the place, Mr. Henderson, he's been there since I was a boy. He's got to be pushing ninety now, still working the cash register every day. He wears these thick glasses, the kind that make his eyes look huge. He told me the spray would work, but it just made the grass turn brown in patches. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins—the one who brought the tuna casserole—she told me I should just dig them out by the roots with a special tool. She ordered one from a catalog, one of those thick paper ones that comes in the mail. I used to love looking through the Sears Roebuck catalog when I was little. The toy section in the back was the best part. I wanted a shiny red wagon so badly one year, and my father actually got it for me for Christmas. We didn't have much money, so I know he must have saved up for months. I pulled that wagon everywhere. I used to give my little sister rides down the sidewalk... but, um, what were we talking about? It's like... it's like I'm shoveling it in before I even realize what I'm doing. Fast, just really fast. My wife, Mary—God rest her soul—she used to say I ate like a man who hadn't seen a hot meal in a week. She was a slow eater, Mary was. Took her time with every bite. We'd go to this little diner on Main Street, used to be called Sal's, I think it's a hardware store now. I remember when they used to show double features there for a quarter. Saw 'The Bridge on the River Kwai' there... magnificent picture. But anyway, my logs in that notebook, they show that for months now, almost every single week, there's at least one afternoon where I just lose it. Eat everything in sight. Sometimes I just push myself to the bone, walking miles around the neighborhood or doing heavy yard work until my back aches. Which reminds me, the gutters on my house are just full of leaves again. That oak tree in the front yard drops them everywhere. I told the kid down the street I'd pay him ten bucks to clear them out, but kids these days, they don't want to work. Back when I was his age, I'd have jumped at ten bucks. Weight's a funny thing, isn't it? You know, back when I was working at the plant—forty years I put in there, stamping metal parts—I never had to worry about my weight. Charlie was a big guy, though. He always had a gut on him, even when we were young. He used to eat these massive hero sandwiches for lunch, wrapped in that thick white paper. I wonder whatever happened to Charlie... he moved out to Arizona, I think. Said the dry heat was better for his joints. My knees have been giving me some trouble lately, actually. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose... I mean, the weight is the whole reason. If I didn't worry about gaining it, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to do all that. But I do worry, constantly. Even with my regular doctor telling me I’m too thin, if I step on that scale in the morning and it's up just half a pound, I get so panicky. I feel just terribly ashamed, like I’ve completely let myself go, and I feel like I just have to fix it right away. 

Half a pound... you know, that's exactly how much sliced ham I always used to ask for at the deli counter down at the old A&P grocery store. Half a pound of the honey-baked ham, sliced paper-thin. The butcher there, his name was Sal, he always knew exactly how I liked it. He had this big, thick mustache that covered his whole upper lip, looked just like a walrus. He wore a white apron that always had a little bit of blood on it, which used to scare my daughter when she was little. She'd hide behind my leg whenever we went up to the meat counter. The A&P closed down years ago, of course. Replaced by one of those giant super-centers where you can buy car tires and groceries in the same place. I don't like those big stores at all. The fluorescent lights give me a terrible headache, and the aisles are so long you need a map just to find the canned peas. I used to grow my own peas, actually, back when I had a proper garden. Sugar snap peas. They climbed right up this trellis I built out of some old chicken wire and cedar posts. Cedar is a good, sturdy wood, doesn't rot easily in the rain. You can smell a good piece of cedar from a mile away... but, oh, excuse me. I'm rambling again, aren't I? What were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. Yes, if the scale goes up even that half a pound, I just panic and feel so ashamed. I feel like I have to fix it immediately, so I get rid of the food. That's really what drives it all.

My wife, Mary—God rest her soul—she used to say I ate like a man who hadn't seen a hot meal in a week. She was a slow eater, Mary was. Took her time with every bite. We'd go to this little diner on Main Street, used to be called Sal's, I think it's a hardware store now. Sal made the best meatloaf, and Mary would just sit there, cutting it into these tiny little pieces. I'd be done with my whole plate and half a slice of cherry pie before she even finished her vegetables. I've been keeping track, you see, writing it all down in this little blue notebook my daughter got me. She bought it at that stationery shop downtown, the one next to the old movie theater. I remember when they used to show double features there for a quarter. Saw 'The Bridge on the River Kwai' there... magnificent picture. But anyway, my logs in that notebook, they show that for months now, almost every single week, there's at least one afternoon where I just lose it. Eat everything in sight. Sometimes I just push myself to the bone, walking miles around the neighborhood or doing heavy yard work until my back aches. Which reminds me, the gutters on my house are just full of leaves again. That oak tree in the front yard drops them everywhere. I told the kid down the street I'd pay him ten bucks to clear them out, but kids these days, they don't want to work. Back when I was his age, I'd have jumped at ten bucks. I used to deliver papers, rain or shine... but yes, the eating and then trying to undo it, it's been happening pretty much every week for a long while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do worry about it, yes. Weight's a funny thing, isn't it? You know, back when I was working at the plant—forty years I put in there, stamping metal parts—I never had to worry about my weight. I was on my feet all day, sweating in the summer... the ventilation in that place was terrible. My buddy Charlie used to bring in this big industrial fan, but it just blew the hot air around. Charlie was a big guy, though. He always had a gut on him, even when we were young. He used to eat these massive hero sandwiches for lunch, wrapped in that thick white paper. I wonder whatever happened to Charlie... he moved out to Arizona, I think. Said the dry heat was better for his joints. My knees have been giving me some trouble lately, actually. The doctor gave me some cream for it, but it smells like wintergreen. Reminds me of those little pink candies my grandmother used to keep in a glass dish on her coffee table. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. I guess I just try not to think about it too much, but it's hard not to when you feel so full.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, guilt... I don't know if guilt is exactly the word for it. It's more just this awful, heavy feeling, you know? Like I've done something I shouldn't have. Speaking of things I shouldn't have done, I bought this new lawnmower last week. One of those fancy self-propelled ones. The salesman at Sears—well, it wasn't Sears, Sears closed down years ago. It was that big box store out on Highway 9. Anyway, the young fella there talked me into it. Said it would be easier on my back. But the darn thing is so complicated to start! You have to prime it three times, hold down this safety bar, and pull the cord all at once. My old push mower, you just gave it a yank and it roared to life. Had that old Briggs & Stratton engine. Built like a tank, that thing was. I used to mow the whole neighborhood's lawns when I was a teenager to save up for my first car. A '57 Chevy Bel Air. Two-tone blue and white. Beautiful machine. Wish I still had it, they go for a fortune at those classic car auctions now. I watch them on the television sometimes on Sunday afternoons when there's no baseball on. But... yeah, I'm not really sure if I'd call it guilt after the eating. Just... bad. I feel bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Unable to stop... yes, that's exactly it, and that's the worst part of the whole thing. What scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just have a mind of their own. They just keep reaching for more, even while I’m sitting there telling myself, Tom, stop this right now. But they just keep moving. \n\nMy hands, I mean. They aren't as quick as they used to be, though. The arthritis really gets into the knuckles these days, especially when it rains. And we've had a lot of rain this spring. My wife, God rest her soul, she used to buy me a new tie every Father's Day. I had quite the collection going by the end. Silk ones, wool ones, even one with little mallard ducks on it. I actually wore that duck tie to my retirement dinner. The boys at the plant threw it for me at the Italian place down on Main Street, the one with the red checkered tablecloths. Best garlic bread I ever had, though I can't eat garlic like I used to. Gives me terrible heartburn now. I have to sleep propped up on three pillows if I even look at a garlic clove... but anyway, what was the question again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what triggers these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, yes, the weight is a big part of it. It's funny you mention the fear, because my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he's a nice enough fellow, a bit young, maybe just out of medical school—he keeps telling me I'm too thin. He looks at my chart and shakes his head. But he doesn't understand. If I step on that scale in the bathroom and it's up even half a pound, I just... I feel this panic in my chest. I feel so ashamed, like I've completely let myself go and I have to fix it right that second. \n\nSpeaking of fixing things, the scale itself is getting a bit wonky. It's one of those old analog ones, you know, with the little dial that spins around. I bought it at a hardware store back in... oh, it must have been 1982. I remember driving down Miller's Hill—steepest road in town, they don't make roads like that anymore—and I was stomping on the pedal, but the truck just kept rolling. Terrifying feeling. He had this little poodle that used to bark at the mailman... but anyway, yeah, it's that same feeling. Just rolling down the hill and I can't stop my hands from grabbing more food, no matter how hard I tell myself to quit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yeah. It's like... it's like I'm shoveling it in before I even realize what I'm doing. Fast, just really fast. My wife, Mary—God rest her soul—she used to say I ate like a man who hadn't seen a hot meal in a week. She was a slow eater, Mary was. Took her time with every bite. We'd go to this little diner on Main Street, used to be called Sal's, I think it's a hardware store now. Sal made the best meatloaf, and Mary would just sit there, cutting it into these tiny little pieces. I'd be done with my whole plate and half a slice of cherry pie before she even finished her vegetables. She always said, \"Tom, the food ain't running away.\" But during these... these times now, it's not like that. It's not just eating fast because I'm hungry. It's a different kind of fast. But Mary, she always knew how to make a meal last an hour...\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating without control happen to you regularly?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do. I've been keeping track, you see, writing it all down in this little blue notebook my daughter got me. She bought it at that stationery shop downtown, the one next to the old movie theater. I remember when they used to show double features there for a quarter. Saw 'The Bridge on the River Kwai' there... magnificent picture. But anyway, my logs in that notebook, they show that for months now, almost every single week, there's at least one afternoon where I just lose it. Eat everything in sight. And then... well, then I panic. I try to fix it. I make myself sick, or take laxatives, or I just don't eat anything at all the next day. Sometimes I just push myself to the bone, walking miles around the neighborhood or doing heavy yard work until my back aches. Which reminds me, the gutters on my house are just full of leaves again. That oak tree in the front yard drops them everywhere. I told the kid down the street I'd pay him ten bucks to clear them out, but kids these days, they don't want to work. Back when I was his age, I'd have jumped at ten bucks. I used to deliver papers, rain or shine... but yes, the eating and then trying to undo it, it's been happening pretty much every week for a long while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do worry about it, yes. Weight's a funny thing, isn't it? You know, back when I was working at the plant—forty years I put in there, stamping metal parts—I never had to worry about my weight. I was on my feet all day, sweating in the summer... the ventilation in that place was terrible. My buddy Charlie used to bring in this big industrial fan, but it just blew the hot air around. Charlie was a big guy, though. He always had a gut on him, even when we were young. He used to eat these massive hero sandwiches for lunch, wrapped in that thick white paper. I wonder whatever happened to Charlie... he moved out to Arizona, I think. Said the dry heat was better for his joints. My knees have been giving me some trouble lately, actually. The doctor gave me some cream for it, but it smells like wintergreen. Reminds me of those little pink candies my grandmother used to keep in a glass dish on her coffee table. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. I guess I just try not to think about it too much, but it's hard not to when you feel so full.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, guilt... I don't know if guilt is exactly the word for it. It's more just this awful, heavy feeling, you know? Like I've done something I shouldn't have. Speaking of things I shouldn't have done, I bought this new lawnmower last week. One of those fancy self-propelled ones. The salesman at Sears—well, it wasn't Sears, Sears closed down years ago. It was that big box store out on Highway 9. Anyway, the young fella there talked me into it. Said it would be easier on my back. But the darn thing is so complicated to start! You have to prime it three times, hold down this safety bar, and pull the cord all at once. My old push mower, you just gave it a yank and it roared to life. Had that old Briggs & Stratton engine. Built like a tank, that thing was. I used to mow the whole neighborhood's lawns when I was a teenager to save up for my first car. A '57 Chevy Bel Air. Two-tone blue and white. Beautiful machine. Wish I still had it, they go for a fortune at those classic car auctions now. I watch them on the television sometimes on Sunday afternoons when there's no baseball on. But... yeah, I'm not really sure if I'd call it guilt after the eating. Just... bad. I feel bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Unable to stop... yes, that's exactly it, and that's the worst part of the whole thing. What scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just have a mind of their own. They just keep reaching for more, even while I’m sitting there telling myself, Tom, stop this right now. But they just keep moving. \n\nMy hands, I mean. They aren't as quick as they used to be, though. The arthritis really gets into the knuckles these days, especially when it rains. And we've had a lot of rain this spring. More than usual, the weatherman on Channel 4 was saying just the other night. I like him, that young fellow with the bright ties. He always wears a different tie. My wife, God rest her soul, she used to buy me a new tie every Father's Day. I had quite the collection going by the end. Silk ones, wool ones, even one with little mallard ducks on it. I actually wore that duck tie to my retirement dinner. The boys at the plant threw it for me at the Italian place down on Main Street, the one with the red checkered tablecloths. Best garlic bread I ever had, though I can't eat garlic like I used to. Gives me terrible heartburn now. I have to sleep propped up on three pillows if I even look at a garlic clove... but anyway, what was the question again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what triggers these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, yes, the weight is a big part of it. It's funny you mention the fear, because my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he's a nice enough fellow, a bit young, maybe just out of medical school—he keeps telling me I'm too thin. He looks at my chart and shakes his head. But he doesn't understand. If I step on that scale in the bathroom and it's up even half a pound, I just... I feel this panic in my chest. I feel so ashamed, like I've completely let myself go and I have to fix it right that second. \n\nSpeaking of fixing things, the scale itself is getting a bit wonky. It's one of those old analog ones, you know, with the little dial that spins around. I bought it at a hardware store back in... oh, it must have been 1982. The same year I put the new roof on the house. We used asphalt shingles, the heavy-duty ones. The roofer said they'd last thirty years, and he wasn't lying, though the wind took a few off during that big storm we had last October. The one that knocked the power out for three days. I had to use a flashlight to get around the house, and the batteries kept dying. I always buy the cheap batteries at the dollar store, but my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she swears by the name-brand ones. She brought over a casserole when the power was out. Tuna noodle, I think it was. It had those little crushed potato chips on top. I used to love those when I was a kid... but, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the scale. Yes, if it goes up even a little, I just panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to make yourself vomit after these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I do. After I eat like that, I'll go into the bathroom and, you know, make myself bring it all back up. Sometimes I'll even take laxatives, too, because I just get so terrified that the food is going to stay inside me and turn into fat. \n\nSpeaking of the bathroom, I had to call the plumber out just last Tuesday. The sink was draining so slow, you wouldn't believe it. Took ten minutes just for the water to go down after I shaved. I use one of those old safety razors, you know, the ones where you have to put the double-edged blade in yourself. My father taught me how to shave with one of those when I was fifteen. He always used Williams Mug shaving soap and a badger hair brush. Smelled like... well, it smelled like my dad. He worked at the lumber yard down by the train tracks for forty years. Hard worker, he was. Never took a sick day. The trains used to rattle the windows of our house when they went by at night. I found it soothing, actually, like a lullaby. The freight trains were the longest. You'd count the cars—fifty, sixty, sometimes seventy cars long, carrying coal and lumber and who knows what else. They don't run as many trains on that line anymore. It's mostly trucks now. Big eighteen-wheelers tearing up the highway. The noise they make on the interstate is just awful, especially when they use those engine brakes. Jake brakes, I think my nephew called them. He drives a truck. Hauls produce from California all the way to New York. Brings me a crate of fresh oranges sometimes when he passes through. Best oranges you'll ever taste, so sweet and juicy... but, um, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, I do make myself throw up, or take the laxatives. I just can't stand the thought of it staying in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think making yourself vomit or using laxatives after eating helps prevent you from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that's exactly why I do it. I mean, I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it all back up, or I take those laxatives, because I'm just so scared that if I don't, all that food will just stay right there in my stomach and turn into fat. I just can't have that. \n\nThough, you know, speaking of stomachs, mine has been a bit upset lately anyway. I think it might be that new brand of coffee I bought at the supermarket. I usually stick to Folgers, the classic roast in the red plastic tub. My wife always bought Folgers. She'd set the percolator up every night before bed, so all we had to do was plug it in when we woke up. The smell of that coffee brewing in the morning... it was heavenly. We had this little breakfast nook in our old house, with a bay window that looked out over the backyard. We had a bird feeder out there, a wooden one shaped like a little barn. The blue jays were always bullying the smaller birds away from the seeds. Nasty things, blue jays, though they are pretty to look at. My grandson, little Jimmy—well, he's not so little anymore, he's in college now, studying engineering of all things—he used to love watching those birds. He'd sit there with his nose pressed against the glass, pointing at the cardinals and the chickadees. He wants to build bridges, he says. Or maybe airplanes, I can't quite remember. He's a smart boy, gets it from his mother's side. My daughter was always top of her class in math... but, oh, what was the question? Right, the weight. Yes, I do it to stop the food from staying inside me, to stop the weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your attempts to vomit or use laxatives after eating are the only things that keep you from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not just those things. I mean, after a really bad eating spell, I'll... I'll make myself skip breakfast and lunch the next day, just drink black coffee. And then I'll go out and keep walking laps around the neighborhood, or I'll do yard chores, just raking and weeding long after my knees and my back are screaming at me to stop. I just have to burn it off, you see. \n\nSpeaking of yard chores, the dandelions this year are just out of control. I don't know what it is about the soil in my front yard, but they just spring up overnight like little yellow buttons. I tried that spray stuff, the one in the green bottle you hook up to the garden hose? Bought it at the hardware store downtown. The fellow who runs the place, Mr. Henderson, he's been there since I was a boy. He's got to be pushing ninety now, still working the cash register every day. He wears these thick glasses, the kind that make his eyes look huge. He told me the spray would work, but it just made the grass turn brown in patches. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins—the one who brought the tuna casserole—she told me I should just dig them out by the roots with a special tool. She ordered one from a catalog, one of those thick paper ones that comes in the mail. I used to love looking through the Sears Roebuck catalog when I was little. The toy section in the back was the best part. I wanted a shiny red wagon so badly one year, and my father actually got it for me for Christmas. We didn't have much money, so I know he must have saved up for months. I pulled that wagon everywhere. I used to give my little sister rides down the sidewalk... but, um, what were we talking about? Oh, right. The walking and the yard work. Yes, I do that to make up for the eating, too. Just push myself until I can't anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to vomit or use laxatives after eating if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, the weight is the whole reason. If I didn't worry about gaining it, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to do all that. But I do worry, constantly. Even with my regular doctor telling me I’m too thin, if I step on that scale in the morning and it's up just half a pound, I get so panicky. I feel just terribly ashamed, like I’ve completely let myself go, and I feel like I just have to fix it right away. \n\nHalf a pound... you know, that's exactly how much sliced ham I always used to ask for at the deli counter down at the old A&P grocery store. Half a pound of the honey-baked ham, sliced paper-thin. The butcher there, his name was Sal, he always knew exactly how I liked it. He had this big, thick mustache that covered his whole upper lip, looked just like a walrus. He wore a white apron that always had a little bit of blood on it, which used to scare my daughter when she was little. She'd hide behind my leg whenever we went up to the meat counter. The A&P closed down years ago, of course. Replaced by one of those giant super-centers where you can buy car tires and groceries in the same place. I don't like those big stores at all. The fluorescent lights give me a terrible headache, and the aisles are so long you need a map just to find the canned peas. I used to grow my own peas, actually, back when I had a proper garden. Sugar snap peas. They climbed right up this trellis I built out of some old chicken wire and cedar posts. Cedar is a good, sturdy wood, doesn't rot easily in the rain. You can smell a good piece of cedar from a mile away... but, oh, excuse me. I'm rambling again, aren't I? What were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. Yes, if the scale goes up even that half a pound, I just panic and feel so ashamed. I feel like I have to fix it immediately, so I get rid of the food. That's really what drives it all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
